### flex2-overview
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: overview and data limits
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv; investigation summary text supplied by the user

FLEX-2 extends the FLEX results to fuels and conditions closer to real combustors and provides benchmark data on bi-component droplet combustion for validating simulations and building new theoretical models. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
This file holds 470 chunks: 406 PDF page chunks, 8 experimental record chunks, 36 ISA-Tab metadata chunks, 5 chunks from the investigation summary (objectives, approach, hypothesis, research impacts), 12 conceptual synthesis chunks, 1 dataset summary chunks, plus this overview, the glossary and the limits above. ### flex2-glossary
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: columns, terms and abbreviations
Sources: Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv; investigation summary text supplied by the user

Surrogate: a mixture of pure fuels that simulates the behavior of a real fuel; FLEX-2 data are meant to support their development. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
Independently varied parameters: fuel type, ambient gas composition, ambient pressure, droplet initial diameter, droplet translation velocity (and acceleration), and droplet spacing and initial size in a binary linear array. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
Columns in PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv (8 records), exactly as named in the source apart from extra spaces: Sample (Fuel Type); Initial Droplet Diameter (mm); Ambient Gas Mixture; Oxygen Mole Fraction; Ambient Pressure (atm); Ambient Temperature (C); Flow Velocity (cm/s). [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv; column names with extra spaces removed]

### flex2-objectives
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Objectives
Sources: Investigation summary text supplied by the user

The primary goal of the FLEX-2 flight experiments is to extend the results of FLEX-1 to fuels and environmental conditions that mimic real combustor conditions, as well as, provide benchmark experimental data on bi-component droplet combustion over a wide range of initial conditions in order to validate numerical simulations and to develop new theoretical models. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
Detailed experimental objectives can be further itemized as: [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
1. Investigate the role of liquid-phase mixing on the burning characteristics of bi-component fuel droplets. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
2. Examine the steady and unsteady liquid and gas-phase phenomena in pure fuel combustion. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
3. Study flame extinction mechanisms and their boundaries. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
4. Analyze the soot formation process, including soot volume fraction soot-shell dynamics. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
5. Determine the influence of droplet interactions on the flammability limits and extinction behavior of droplet arrays. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
6. Validate theoretical models of droplet combustion through comprehensive experimental data collection in microgravity environments. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]

-----
### flex2-approach-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Approach [part 1/2]
Sources: Investigation summary text supplied by the user

The chamber was filled with the appropriate atmosphere, which was varied in pressure between 0.5 atm to 3.0 atm and O2 concentration between 0.1 to 0.4 mole fraction with the diluent gas making up the balance. A settling time of approximately 2 minutes elapsed prior to initiating the test in order to ensure that the temperature and pressure of the chamber gases have stabilized. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
This settling time was followed by the dispensing of a predetermined amount of fuel (based on the target droplet size) onto the support fiber for those tests in which the droplet is not freely deployed. When sufficient fuel has been dispensed the dispensing needles were retracted and a dwell period of at least 10 seconds was allowed for the droplet internal fluid motion induced by deployment to subside. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
This was then followed by initiating power to the ignitor for a selectable amount of time ranging from 1 second to 5 seconds after which the ignitor was retracted from the field of view. If the flow field is to be generated by translating the droplet then droplet motion would commence at the same time that the ignitor is retracted. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
A near real-time download of the color camera video was employed in order to verify successful droplet deployment, ignition, and overall progress of the experiment. Pressure and temperature data of the chamber environment was also recorded in near real time. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]

-----
### flex2-approach-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Approach [part 2/2]
Sources: Investigation summary text supplied by the user

The overall investigation approach can be summarized as: [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
1. Test parameters are determined, including pressure, temperature, fuel mixture, and humidity. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
2. Fill the combustion chamber with the selected gas mixtures. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
3. Deploy the droplet(s) carefully controlling the droplet diameter. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
4. Allow the droplet to stabilize in the microgravity environment. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
5. Ignite the droplet in a uniform and controlled fashion. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
6. Monitor combustions and track flame evolution using high speed photography. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
7. Monitor and adjust experimental conditions as needed. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
8. Analyze data. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
The independently varied experimental parameters during FLEX-2 experiments were 1) Fuel type 2) Ambient gas composition 3) Ambient Pressure 4) Droplet initial diameter 5) Droplet translation velocity (and acceleration) 6) Droplet spacing and initial size in a binary linear array. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]

-----
### flex2-hypothesis
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Hypothesis
Sources: Investigation summary text supplied by the user

Fairly sophisticated numerical simulations of convective droplet combustion have been developed in the last decade where the key contribution is the inclusion of full Navier-Stokes equations for the gas-phase flow field. However, due to the inherent complexity of the problem, varying degrees of approximations have been made in treating the chemistry, radiation, and the coupling between the gas and liquid phases. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
The validity of the assumptions or the hypothesis used in these simulation have not been confirmed by using benchmark data. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
By conducting these experiments in a microgravity environment, the effects of buoyancy are minimized, and the droplet can be characterized only as a function of thermocapillary or solutalcapillary stresses allowing for a clear observation of the fundamental combustion processes, The present FLEX-2 experiments using bi-component mixtures. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
In addition, the microgravity environment allows study of such phenomena as the transitions between radiative and diffusive extension of the flame. The primary goal of the flight experiments is to provide benchmark experimental data on bi-component droplet combustion over a wide range of initial conditions that could be used to validate numerical simulations and to develop new theoretical models. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
Note on the source text: Source text has a garbled sentence ("The present FLEX-2 experiments using bi-component mixtures") and "extension of the flame" likely means "extinction". Wording preserved. [Source: note added during corpus preparation]

-----
### flex2-research-impacts
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Research Impacts / Earth Benefits
Sources: Investigation summary text supplied by the user

The combustion of liquid fuels is the overwhelming energy source in the transportation sector. Insights gained from these studies can lead to more efficient and cleaner combustion technologies which are crucial for reducing pollution and greenhouse gas emissions. By improving fuel efficiency and developing better fuels, these experiments can contribute to: [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
1) More sustainable energy production. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
2) Future combustor designs with minimal carbon footprint, maximum fuel efficiency and less pollutant emissions. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
3) Allow quantitative evaluation of the performance of future fuels in combustors through the development of surrogates (mixtures of pure fuels that simulate the behavior of real fuels). [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]
4) Validation of jet engine models by engine manufacturers with support from prior droplet results. [Source: investigation summary text supplied by the user]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-001-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Purpose and scope of the knowledge base (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf; CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf

This knowledge base describes four related but distinct microgravity combustion research efforts: FLEX (Flame Extinguishment Experiment), FLEX-2 (Fundamental Studies in Droplet Combustion and Flame Extinguishment), CFI (Cool Flames Investigation), and CFI-G (Cool Flames Investigation with Gases). [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
They share the use of the International Space Station (ISS), the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), and controlled combustion experiments, but they do not study identical flame configurations or answer identical scientific questions. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
FLEX and FLEX-2 primarily use individual liquid-fuel droplets to investigate burning, flame extinction, flammability limits, suppressant performance, soot, and radiation. CFI extends the droplet-combustion work toward low-temperature, quasi-steady cool-flame behavior using heavier liquid fuels and controlled pressure and diluent conditions. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
CFI-G studies gaseous-fuel diffusion flames supported by spherical porous burners, including both normal and inverse flame configurations. The distinction between liquid-droplet flames and burner-supported gaseous diffusion flames must be preserved in any retrieval system. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The source collection contains science reports, engineering and science requirements documents, operational protocols, experimental tables, and a multi-sheet CFI data workbook. These document types have different evidentiary roles. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-001-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Purpose and scope of the knowledge base (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf; CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf

Requirements documents describe intended capabilities, test matrices, and success criteria; operational reports describe what was actually performed; science reports summarize observations and interpretations; experimental tables contain individual test records or planned parameter ranges. A model should not treat a planned condition as proof that the condition was tested, nor treat a hypothesis as an established result. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The reports are time-bounded documents. Some explicitly state that data analysis was preliminary or still underway. When answering questions, identify whether a statement is a proposed objective, a hypothesis, an operational fact, a preliminary observation, or a final measured result. If sources disagree, preserve the disagreement and cite the relevant document rather than silently choosing one value. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

### flex2-shared-comb-006-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 scope and relationship to FLEX (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; Flex/FLEX Engineering Requirements Document.pdf; Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv

FLEX-2 is titled Fundamental Studies in Droplet Combustion and Flame Extinguishment in Microgravity. It builds on the earlier FLEX work but has its own science requirements, engineering requirements, test matrix, and data records. It should not be treated as merely another name for FLEX. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
Its stated science program includes detailed measurements of droplet combustion, flame extinction, radiation, soot, and the influence of ambient conditions and controlled droplet motion. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The FLEX-2 Science Requirements Document identifies objectives that include determining diffusive and radiative flame-extinction boundaries for selected fuels, measuring droplet extinction diameters for model validation, measuring soot volume fractions and soot temperatures, measuring radiant emissions from luminous and non-luminous flames, and characterizing burning-rate behavior. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
It also includes investigations of binary fuel mixtures and the influence of droplet translation relative to the surrounding gas. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The FLEX-2 test-matrix CSV lists candidate fuels including pure iso-octane, n-octane, heptane, decane, ethanol, a propanol/glycerol mixture, and a heptane/iso-octane mixture. The table specifies initial droplet diameters of approximately 1.5–5 mm, ambient mixtures of O2/N2 or O2/He, oxygen mole fractions of 0.17, 0.21, 0.25, and 0.30, pressures of 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, or 3.0 atm, ambient temperature of 25 C, and flow velocities from 0.1 to 5 cm/s. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

### flex2-shared-comb-006-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 scope and relationship to FLEX (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; Flex/FLEX Engineering Requirements Document.pdf; Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv

These values describe the test matrix represented in the supplied CSV. They should be treated as listed test conditions or planned ranges unless an associated experimental record confirms that a specific combination was actually run. The table is not itself a results dataset. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
For retrieval, questions about “what FLEX-2 planned to vary” should be answered from this matrix and requirements documents, while questions about observed outcomes require the corresponding experimental data or final science analysis. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

### flex2-shared-comb-007-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Droplet deployment, support, ignition, and chamber systems (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX Engineering Requirements Document.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; Flex/FLEX Overall Ext File-2.pdf

The FLEX-2 engineering requirements describe the Multi-user Droplet Combustion Apparatus (MDCA) as the experiment platform operating within the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR). The apparatus includes a chamber insert assembly and internal systems for fuel dispensing, droplet deployment, fiber support, ignition, cooling, radiation measurement, avionics, data control, power distribution, and diagnostics. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
These subsystems are interdependent: a reproducible droplet must be dispensed and positioned, the chamber atmosphere must be established, ignition must occur at the intended location, and the diagnostic systems must capture the event. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The fuel dispenser meters a predetermined amount of liquid fuel to establish a target droplet size. The deployment system positions or releases the droplet into the observation region. Depending on the test, the droplet may be freely floating or supported by a very small fiber. A support fiber can reduce droplet drift and keep the droplet within the diagnostic field of view, but it can also introduce thermal conduction and other boundary effects. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The science program therefore includes comparisons of supported and free-floating droplets where possible. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-007-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Droplet deployment, support, ignition, and chamber systems (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX Engineering Requirements Document.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; Flex/FLEX Overall Ext File-2.pdf

The ignition system uses heated igniters to initiate combustion. The igniter must be positioned near the droplet for ignition and then removed from the diagnostic field of view so that it does not interfere with flame observations. Cooling and thermal-management systems protect apparatus components and maintain the intended operating environment. Radiometers and camera systems provide synchronized diagnostic measurements. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The operational sequence includes chamber conditioning, droplet dispensing and deployment, a settling period where appropriate, ignition, igniter withdrawal, combustion observation, and post-extinction observation. Operational records may include failed deployment, failed ignition, droplet motion, camera problems, or other anomalies. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
These are not merely administrative notes: they can determine whether a test is suitable for quantitative analysis. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
When building a RAG knowledge base, distinguish engineering capability from science outcome. A requirement that a subsystem “shall” perform a function is not evidence that every test achieved that function. Likewise, an observed anomaly in an individual test should not be generalized into a permanent limitation of the apparatus. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-016-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: How FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G differ (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf; CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf

FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G form a related research sequence, but their experimental systems and scientific emphasis differ. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
FLEX investigates the extinction of burning liquid droplets and the efficacy of gaseous fire suppressants. Its principal outcomes include flammability boundaries, limiting oxygen index, extinction diameter, burning histories, radiation, soot behavior, and model-validation data. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
FLEX-2 extends fundamental droplet-combustion and extinction studies. Its science requirements include extinction boundaries, soot and radiation measurements, fuel comparisons and mixtures, and controlled droplet translation. Its supplied test-matrix file lists fuels and ranges of droplet diameter, oxygen concentration, pressure, and flow velocity. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
CFI follows the FLEX investigations to focus on low-temperature cool-flame combustion around liquid droplets. It investigates n-dodecane, n-dodecane/iso-dodecane mixtures, and farnesane, with variations in pressure and helium dilution. Its key measurements include droplet regression, hot- and cool-flame diameters, extinction behavior, and radiant emission. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
CFI-G shifts from liquid droplets to gaseous fuels supplied through spherical porous burners. It studies normal and inverse cool diffusion flames, fuel and oxidizer flow conditions, dilution, pressure, and flame transitions. Its central modeling emphasis is CFD and cool-flame chemical kinetics for spherical gaseous diffusion flames. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-016-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: How FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G differ (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex/FLEX_EIRD_v4.pdf; Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf; CFI/CFI Science Final Report.pdf; CDFI/PSI-159_Reports_FinalRpt_ACME.CFI-G.pdf

The common scientific themes are microgravity transport, spherical flame structure, extinction, low-temperature chemistry, radiation, soot, experimental diagnostics, and validation of predictive models. The central difference is the physical configuration: single liquid droplets in FLEX/FLEX-2/CFI versus burner-supported gaseous diffusion flames in CFI-G. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
A language model should not transfer a numerical range, fuel list, mechanism, or observation from one investigation to another unless the source explicitly supports that transfer. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

### flex2-shared-comb-017-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: How to interpret the supplied experimental files safely (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex/PSI-69_Experimental table_FLEX.csv; Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv; CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx; CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Normal Flames.csv; CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Inverse Flames.csv

The experimental files have different structures and should be indexed with investigation-specific metadata. The FLEX CSV is a test-level dataset with one row per test and fields describing fuel, date/time, pressure, ambient gas composition, droplet size, visible flame extinction diameter, burning rate, burn time, and test end. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
The FLEX-2 CSV supplied here is a test-matrix table of fuels and parameter ranges, not a row-by-row record of completed tests. The CFI workbook is a multi-sheet dataset with test metadata, measured analysis fields, camera settings, igniter settings, and qualitative comments. The CFI-G normal and inverse CSVs are separate test-level tables with different columns. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
Recommended metadata for each indexed chunk or row include: investigation name; experiment configuration; source filename; source document type; page, sheet, or row identifier; test identifier where available; fuel; pressure and unit; oxygen mole fraction; diluent or suppressant; droplet diameter or burner flow condition; flame configuration; and whether the content is a requirement, planned matrix, observation, or interpreted finding. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
Do not convert units silently. For example, the FLEX table uses pressure in mmHg, the CFI-G tables use approximate pressure in bar, and the CFI workbook uses atm. Store the original value and unit, and add a normalized value only as a separate, explicitly computed field. Preserve the source column name as well as any normalized schema name. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

-----
### flex2-shared-comb-017-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: How to interpret the supplied experimental files safely (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex/PSI-69_Experimental table_FLEX.csv; Flex2/PSI-68_Experimental table_Flex2.csv; CFI/CFI_DataSummary_Spreadsheet_Jan2021.xlsx; CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Normal Flames.csv; CDFI/PSI-159_Experimental table_CFI-G_Inverse Flames.csv

Missing values must remain null or blank. Do not replace them with zero, “false,” or an inferred result. Qualitative comments should be retained because they may explain why a test was unsuccessful or why a measurement should be excluded. Test-end labels and abbreviations should be preserved verbatim until a source-backed definition is available. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]
For retrieval quality, keep individual test records small and atomic, but include enough surrounding metadata to make each record interpretable. For explanatory narrative chunks, use one coherent topic per chunk and repeat essential context such as the investigation name. This reduces the risk that a retrieved passage about a droplet flame will be mistaken for a CFI-G burner flame. [Source: conceptual synthesis; see Sources above]

### flex2-shared-guide-rag-001-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Corpus scope, source hierarchy, and interpretation rules (shared across investigations) [part 1/2]
Sources: Data.zip

This corpus is based on the uploaded Data.zip archive. It covers four distinct investigations: FLEX, FLEX-2, CFI, and CFI-G. FLEX, FLEX-2, and CFI concern liquid-fuel droplet combustion, while CFI-G concerns gaseous-fuel diffusion flames on spherical porous burners. [Source: corpus curation guidance]
The corpus contains source PDF text segmented by page, CSV rows retained as individual experimental records, CFI workbook records grouped by test identifier across sheets, metadata documents extracted from embedded ZIP archives, dataset inventories, and a separate conceptual synthesis layer. [Source: corpus curation guidance]
Evidence categories must remain distinct. Engineering requirements describe intended capabilities. Science requirements and test matrices describe objectives or planned conditions. Operational protocols describe procedures. Experimental tables record test-specific conditions and outcomes. Science reports may describe observations, interpretations, or hypotheses, and some explicitly characterize their analysis as preliminary. [Source: corpus curation guidance]
Never turn a planned test into a completed test, a hypothesis into a finding, or a blank data field into a negative result. [Source: corpus curation guidance]
When answering a scientific question, retrieve the relevant investigation and source document first. For test-specific questions, prefer experimental_record chunks and cite their test identifier and row or workbook location. For methods or objectives, use the relevant requirements or protocol PDF and cite its page. For scientific interpretation, use science reports and retain the report's own qualification and uncertainty. [Source: corpus curation guidance]

Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: Corpus scope, source hierarchy, and interpretation rules (shared across investigations) [part 2/2]
Sources: Data.zip

If sources disagree, report the discrepancy with both source locations rather than silently reconciling it. Preserve original units and column names; any unit conversion should be separately labeled and reproducible. [Source: corpus curation guidance]
The conceptual synthesis chunks are useful for orientation but are secondary to direct source extraction. If a synthesis statement conflicts with a primary source passage or data record, the primary source should control and the conflict should be surfaced. [Source: corpus curation guidance]

### flex2-isa-i-investigation-study-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: ISA-Tab i_Investigation.txt in PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip, STUDY [part 3/35]
Sources: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt

Comment[Approach]: "The chamber was filled with the appropriate atmosphere, which was varied in pressure between 0.5 atm to 3.0 atm and O2 concentration between 0.1 to 0.4 mole fraction with the diluent gas making up the balance. A settling time of approximately 2 minutes elapsed prior to initiating the test in order to ensure that the temperature and pressure of the chamber gases have stabilized. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
This settling time was followed by the dispensing of a predetermined amount of fuel (based on the target droplet size) onto the support fiber for those tests in which the droplet is not freely deployed. When sufficient fuel has been dispensed the dispensing needles were retracted and a dwell period of at least 10 seconds was allowed for the droplet internal fluid motion induced by deployment to subside. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
This was then followed by initiating power to the ignitor for a selectable amount of time ranging from 1 second to 5 seconds after which the ignitor was retracted from the field of view. If the flow field is to be generated by translating the droplet then droplet motion would commence at the same time that the ignitor is retracted. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
A near real-time download of the color camera video was employed in order to verify successful droplet deployment, ignition, and overall progress of the experiment. Pressure and temperature data of the chamber environment was also recorded in near real time. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]

### flex2-isa-i-investigation-study-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: ISA-Tab i_Investigation.txt in PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip, STUDY [part 4/35]
Sources: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt

The overall investigation approach can be summarized as:; 1. Test parameters are determined, including pressure, temperature, fuel mixture, and humidity. 2. Fill the combustion chamber with the selected gas mixtures. 3. Deploy the droplet(s) carefully controlling the droplet diameter. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
4. Allow the droplet to stabilize in the microgravity environment. 5. Ignite the droplet in a uniform and controlled fashion. 6. Monitor combustions and track flame evolution using high speed photography. 7. Monitor and adjust experimental conditions as needed. 8. Analyze data. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
The independently varied experimental parameters during FLEX-2 experiments were 1) Fuel type 2) Ambient gas composition 3) Ambient Pressure 4) Droplet initial diameter 5) Droplet translation velocity (and acceleration) 6) Droplet spacing and initial size in a binary linear array." [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
Comment[DOI]: 10.60555/byp8-h018; Comment[Experiment Hardware]: Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR), Multi-user Droplet Combustion Appartatus (MDCA) [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]

### flex2-isa-i-investigation-study-6
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: ISA-Tab i_Investigation.txt in PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip, STUDY [part 6/35]
Sources: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt

Comment[Hypothesis]: Fairly sophisticated numerical simulations of convective droplet combustion have been developed in the last decade where the key contribution is the inclusion of full Navier-Stokes equations for the gas-phase flow field. However, due to the inherent complexity of the problem, varying degrees of approximations have been made in treating the chemistry, radiation, and the coupling between the gas and liquid phases. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
The validity of the assumptions or the hypothesis used in these simulation have not been confirmed by using benchmark data. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
By conducting these experiments in a microgravity environment, the effects of buoyancy are minimized, and the droplet can be characterized only as a function of thermocapillary or solutalcapillary stresses allowing for a clear observation of the fundamental combustion processes, The present FLEX-2 experiments using bi-component mixtures. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
In addition, the microgravity environment allows study of such phenomena as the transitions between radiative and diffusive extension of the flame. The primary goal of the flight experiments is to provide benchmark experimental data on bi-component droplet combustion over a wide range of initial conditions that could be used to validate numerical simulations and to develop new theoretical models. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]

### flex2-isa-i-investigation-study-7

Comment[Objective]: "The primary goal of the FLEX-2 flight experiments is to extend the results of FLEX-1 to fuels and environmental conditions that mimic real combustor conditions, as well as, provide benchmark experimental data on bi-component droplet combustion over a wide range of initial conditions in order to validate numerical simulations and to develop new theoretical models. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
Detailed experimental objectives can be further itemized as:; 1. Investigate the role of liquid-phase mixing on the burning characteristics of bi-component fuel droplets. 2. Examine the steady and unsteady liquid and gas-phase phenomena in pure fuel combustion. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]
3. Study flame extinction mechanisms and their boundaries. 4. Analyze the soot formation process, including soot volume fraction soot-shell dynamics. [Source: Flex2/PSI-68_metadata_PSI-132_FLEX-2.zip / i_Investigation.txt]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg7-1

1.0 Introduction FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 1 Introduction [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Combustion, which has been both friend and foe to mankind since antiquity, still defies compre-hension in many important respects. Needs therefore exist to advance fundamental knowledge in combustion science. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Practical consequences of such advancement of knowledge are improve-ments in strategies for energy production, since most of the energy that is used by society will continue to be provided by combustion for many years, and mitigation of fire losses, fire safety being a continuing concern in all sectors. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
The NASA Microgravity Combustion Science Program, which was established through recognition of these needs, did much to increase our fundamental understanding of combustion processes Ronney (1988),Williams (2007). Droplet combustion experiments had a great deal to do with the advancements in knowledge through this program. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Such experiments, in effect, focus on a microcosm of the field of combustion that can be used to study most aspects of the topic. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg7-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 7 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

In addition, droplet combustion is of practical interest in itself, since liquid fuels possess high energy content per unit mass and are relatively safe to store and transport, making them valuable to the transportation industry for the foreseeable future. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Phenomena of finite-rate gas-phase chemistry, multicomponent-species gas-phase transport processes, production of soot and other pollutants, phase-change processes, liquid-phase species separation and fluid motion, natural and force convection, radiation and conductive energy transfer and interactions of different burning fuel elements, for example, all are involved in droplet combustion (and, in fact, all are aspects of the subject of the [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
present SRD). By offering a well-controlled arena in which to investigate these combustion phenomena, microgravity droplet combustion research provides valuable data that help develop detailed chemical and physical models of combustion phenomena. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg7-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 7 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The NASA program in the area of microgravity droplet combustion has employed laboratory experiments, low-gravity facilities, aircraft flying parabolic trajectories and space-based research to reduce or eliminate buoyancy, thereby increasing the fidelity of the descriptions of the com-bustion processes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Following glovebox space-shuttle experiments on a fiber-supported droplet combustion, FSDC, the freely deployed droplet-combustion experiment DCE was flown on the MSL-1 missions, providing a number of new insights into the fundamentals of droplet com-bustion. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
Among the DCE results was the demonstration of boundaries between radiative and diffusive extinction of combustion in droplet burning and the establishment of atmospheres in which the dilution was great enough that the oxygen concentration was too low for combustion to occur Nayagam et al. (1998), Ackerman et al. (2003). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg7-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 7 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

This last observation demonstrated how droplet-combustion studies could be used to investigate the limiting oxygen index, LOI, needed to prevent combustion, and so the Flammability-limit experiment FLEX, was designed for the International Space Station, ISS, to be run using the multiuser droplet combustion ap-paratus, MDCA, in the combustion integrated rack, CIR. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
While FLEX is focussed on spacecraft fire safety and fire suppression, FLEX-2 returns to the broader objective of improving the fun-damental understanding of liquid-fuel combustion. The FLEX-2 experiments thus fall in the category of the broad NASA mission to improve basic science through microgravity research. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
They focus on ethanol and decane as fuels, as well as various fuel mixtures, in oxygen-nitrogen and oxygen-helium atmospheres at various pressures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]
1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 7]

[Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 8]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg9-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)

2.0 Literature Review FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 2 Literature Review 2.1 Droplet Combustion and Extinction [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
The spherically symmetrical combustion of a liquid fuel droplet in a quiescent ambient gaseous oxidizing atmosphere is a classical problem in combustion research, having been addressed first more than 50 years ago by Godsave (1952), Hall and Diederichsen (1953) and Spalding (1953b,a). Numerous reviews of the subject are now available in the literature, among them those of Wise and Agoston (1958), Williams, F. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
(1965), Williams (1973),Faeth (1977), Law (1982), Sirignano (1993, 1999), Avedisian (2000), Chiu (2000) and Choi and Dryer (2001). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
An advantage of the spherical symmetry is that only one spatial dimension enters the description of the combustion process, so that one-dimensional (spherically symmetrical) time-dependent conservation equations apply, greatly facilitating both computational and theoretical descrip-tions of the combustion and thereby enhancing understanding of experimental results, which becomes much more difficult, uncertain and inaccurate in multidimensional [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
situations. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg9-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 9 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Natural convection, however, destroys the spherical symmetry of the combustion in normal gravity, as was quite evident already in the earliest experiments of Hall and Diederichsen (1953) and Gold-smith (1956). Kumagai (1956) was the first to realize that microgravity experiments afforded the opportunity to achieve spherical symmetry, a fact which NASA has used to advantage in fundamental investigations for a number of years e.g., (Williams, 1981; [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
Dietrich et al., 1996; Nayagam et al., 1998). Although microgravity experiments are, in a sense, somewhat artificial for earthbound combustion, they are of direct concern for combustion hazards in the spacecraft which are needed to pursue human exploration of planets and their satellites in space. Despite the simplifications afforded by spherical symmetry, many complexities remain in micro-gravity studies of droplet combustion. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
Among them are time-dependent liquid-phase phenom-ena, soot production, radiant energy transfer and effects of multicomponent fuels. These topics are addressed in the following subsections. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg9-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 9 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The focus of the present subsection is on gas-phase phenomena, as they influence burning rates and finite-rate chemistry, leading ultimately to ex-tinction of the spherical flame that surrounds the liquid droplet. Because of the small value of the ratio of gas density to liquid density, in a first approximation the gas phase is quasisteady Williams (1960, 1985). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
It is this quasisteadiness, along with a fast-chemistry approximation, that leads to the well-known "d-square law" for the droplet burning rate, in which the square of the droplet diameter decreases linearly with time. There are, however, notable time-dependent gas-phase phenomena in need of further study, even when the gas-phase chemistry is fast. One such phenomena is the flame-diameter history. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
An entirely quasisteady gas phase with rapid chemistry maintains a constant ratio of flame diameter to droplet diameter during combustion, but already in early microgravity droplet-combustion experiments this was observed not to occur Kumagai and Isoda (1956); Isoda and Kumagai (1958); Okajima and Kumagai (1974a). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg9-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 9 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

It is now understood that whether the flame diameter should behave in a quasisteady manner depends on the value of the stoichiometric mixture fraction, the ratio of the oxidizer mass to the sum of the fuel and oxidizer mass in a stoichiometric mixture (Crespo and Liñan (1975); Waldman (1974); Williams (1985)). A quasisteady flame radius occurs if the stoichiometric [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]
3 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 9]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg10-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 10 [part 1/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

2.1 Droplet Combustion and Extinction FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg10-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 10 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

mixture fraction is sufficiently large, but in most experiments it is too small (less than 0.1) for the flame radius to achieve quasisteady behavior during the droplet lifetime. Dilution of the atmosphere reduces the stoichiometric mixture fraction, as does oxygenation of the fuel, so methanol, for example, in increased-oxygen atmospheres would have a more nearly quasisteady flame radius than hydrocarbon fuels, such as heptane, burning in air. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
Microgravity experiments (e.g., Dietrich et al. (1996)) suggest a stronger tendency toward quasisteady flame-diameter behavior for methanol than for heptane. It is not known, however, whether this tendency also would be exhibited by ethanol, since ethanol is not as highly oxygenated as methanol. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
The proposed experiments with ethanol would help to address this question; for the experiments with decane, the flame diameters should remain unsteady during the entire burning history. Microgravity experiments have shown that there always is an initial period during which the flame diameters increase with time (Nayagam et al. (1998)). During this period the flame resides in the outer transient-diffusive zone (Crespo and Liñan (1975)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg10-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 10 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Only at sufficiently large stoichiometric mixture fractions can it return to the inner convective-diffusive zone and become quasisteady. A tendency for the flame to return, that is, for the flame diameter to begin to decrease with time, can be observed only if the gas-phase chemistry remains sufficiently fast. If the chemistry is too slow, the flame may extinguish before it begins to return. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
Heat losses reduce the temperature of the gas and thereby slows the chemistry. Two types of heat losses are diffusive, that is, by heat conduction away from the flame, and radiative, that is radiant energy loss from the flame region to the surroundings. Diffusive losses are increased at small flame diameters, while radiative losses are increased at large flame diameters relative to energy release. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
Flame extinction that occurs as the flame diameter begins to decrease after reaching a maximum is therefore termed radiative extinction, while extinctions that occurs at small flame diameters are termed diffusive extinction. The boundary between conditions of radiative and diffusive extinction depends on the initial droplet diameter and on the dilution of the atmosphere, as well as on the fuel. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg10-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 10 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Microgravity experiments (Nayagam et al. (1998), Ackerman et al. 2003) have established these boundaries for heptane. The boundaries between radiative and diffusive extinction are, however, not known for ethanol or decane. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
The proposed experiments would determine these boundaries for these two fuels, each of which is of interest, the first as a constituent of biofuels and the second as a constituent of surrogate fuels for diesel and aircraft fuels. Detailed chemical kinetic mechanisms are available for the combustion of both ethanol and decane fuels. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
It is now possible to use this detailed chemistry in time-dependent, spherically symmetrical computations of droplet burning to calculate extinction conditions. However, sys-tematically reduced chemistry models using rate-ratio asymptotics are not yet available for these fuels. Experimental measurement of diffusive and radiative extinction boundaries for the two fuels should help in developing these reduced chemical kinetic schemes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]
4 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 10]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg11-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)

2.2 Liquid-Phase Phenomena FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 2.2 Liquid-Phase Phenomena [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
The bulk of the isolated droplet combustion studies, as seen from the previous section, treat the droplet as being a pure, single component liquid. In contrast, practical fuels are multi-component, or have constituents with a wide range of physical and chemical properties. The gas-phase and thus flame behaviors are determined by the species that have gasified, and liq-uid behaviors are determined by the species that remain inside of the droplets. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
A particular species within a droplet can be gasified only if it is present at the liquid surface. Thus, the gas phase and vaporization behaviors of a fuel droplet are strongly influenced by the rate at which various components are transported to the surface of the droplet. Previous work shows that liquid species diffusion rates are very important in determining the gasification behaviors of multicomponent droplets (see, e.g., Hanson et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
(1982); Randolph et al. (1986); Dwyer, 1989; Megaridis (1993); Sirignano (1999)). A species can reach the surface of the droplet by two mechanisms, diffusion and convection. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg11-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 11 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The causes of internal liquid convection were typically thought to result from the atomization or injection processes and the aerodynamic shear stresses at the droplet surface (Sirignano (1999)). Aharon and Shaw (1996) showed that capillary stresses can promote or oppose droplet internal flows, depending on the relative strengths of the solutal and thermal capillary stresses. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
These capillary stresses can dominate the internal flows and can cause strong random internal motions with spray-size droplets (50 to 100 μm or smaller), even under high droplet velocity conditions representative of modern combustors (Dwyer et al. (1996); Dwyer et al. (1998); Niazmand et al. (1995)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
One of the focuses of the present FLEX-2 experiments will be to investigate the effects of thermal and solutal capillary flows on droplet combustion behaviors. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
The process of differential vaporization of a bi-component fuel is manifested in the gas phase as ‘flame contraction.’ Flame contractions occur as a result of the massive liquid heating (and decreased vaporization) when the surface mass fraction of the low-volatility component rapidly approaches unity. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg11-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 11 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Representative flame and droplet data from Aharon and Shaw (1998) showing a flame contraction are presented in Fig. 1. Theoretical expressions have been developed (Shaw and Williams (1990)) and extended (Aharon and Shaw (1998)) to analyze conditions leading to the onset of flame contractions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
The theory predicts that the ratio of the initial droplet diameter, d0 , to the droplet diameter at the onset of flame contraction, dc , is a function of both the initial mass fraction of the low-volatility component in the droplet, Y, and the parameter ε = 8D/K. This parameter (ε) is the ratio of a characteristic droplet lifetime to a characteristic species diffusion time in a droplet. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
The variable D is the liquid species diffusivity and K is the burning-rate constant prior to the flame contraction. Under typical conditions, ε << 1 is expected; the theory capitalizes on the smallness of ε. The solid lines in Fig. 2 show theoretical predictions of (dc /d0 )3 as a function of Y for various values of ε. Figure 3 shows the effective liquid phase species diffusion coefficients, D, computed from the experiments in Fig. 2. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]
The data in Fig. 3 show that the smallest D values were in the drop tower experiments. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 11]

purpose. The concept of surrogates is not new. As far back as the 1920s surrogate fuels were used to understand the influence of composition on performance of gasoline fuels (Colket et al. (2007)). Over the years, a variety of surrogates have been evaluated. The most elementary is a single component fuel, the relevance of which to a real fuel, is at most to represent single phase heat transfer behavior (Edwards and Maurice (2001)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 13]
More realistically, blends of several fuels are necessary to exploit differences among individual constituents to attempt to reproduce a specific metric of the real fuel. Surrogates containing two (Kyne et al. 1999) to over twenty constituents (Hakansson et al. (2001)) have been proposed to model performance of real fuels including liquid fuels (e.g., sprays Smith et al. (1985)) and gaseous fuels (e.g., Cooke et al. (2005)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 13]
Interestingly, Zhang et al. (2007) used the combustion chemistry of heptane as a building block with additional sub-models to predict acetylene concentration in surrogate mixtures of liquid transportation fuels. Smith et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 13]

Two aspects that can assist development of surrogate fuels are 1) an appropriate experimental configuration for developing data, and 2) identification of appropriate metrics for the chosen configuration to serve as points of comparison between the surrogate and real fuel. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 14]
The choice of combustion configuration in which to develop a surrogate should have the following features: 1) transport is easily modelable; 2) the configuration has a capability to produce accurate data; and 3) combustion incorporates some of the unsteadiness associated with liquid fuel burning processes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 14]
Several experimental configurations satisfy these constraints for gaseous fuels: coun-terflow diffusion flame; shock tube; flow reactor; and pool fire to name a few. All have in common that they allow for a numerically tractable configuration from which complex processes, such as soot formation and complex chemistry, can be examined. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 14]

2.4 Mechanisms of Soot Formation [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg15-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 15 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Understanding soot formation during the combustion of liquid fuels is important due to its negative effects on the environment and human health as well as for its practical uses in certain industrial processes. Despite its practical relevance the process of soot formation during the combustion of a liquid fuel has not been fully understood due to its complexity (Avedisian (2000)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]
The process of soot formation involves both chemical and physical aspects: the chemical aspects involve the reaction pathways for the formation of soot precursors, and the physical aspects include nucleation, soot particle surface growth, oxidation, and particle agglomeration. The most widely used models for soot formations include semi-empirical approaches of Moss et al. (Moss et al. (1988); Bressloff et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]
(1996)), Lindstedt and co-workers (Leung et al. (1991)), and the detailed models proposed by Frenklach and co-workers (Frenklach and Wang (1991, 1994) ). The earliest observations of sooting during spherically symmetric droplet combustion were those of Okajima and Kumagai (1974b, 1982), Yang et al. (1990), Choi et al. (1990a). More recently Jackson and Avedisian (1994) and Nayagam et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg15-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 15 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

(1998) measured soot shell diameter for n-heptane droplets. Choi and Lee (1996); Lee et al. (1998) and Chen and Shaw (2000) have ob-tained soot volume fraction data for droplets of varying initial sizes. The current understanding of sootshell formation suggests that soot particles formed near the flame front are acted upon by viscous drag (caused by Stefan flow), thermophoresis and diffusion. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]
The balance of the various transport mechanisms causes the transient accumulation of soot particles and the formation of a spherical sootshell on the fuel-rich side of the diffusion flame. Further agglomeration of soot within the shell eventually leads to the formation of larger agglomerates that are swept through the diffusion flame by the Stefan drag, and subsequently ignite and burn in the surrounding, hot oxygen rich outer flame zone. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]
9 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 15]

2.4 Mechanisms of Soot Formation FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg16-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 16 [part 2/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Soot formation during combustion of liquid fuels is inherently linked to the combustion process itself and influence the quasi-steady behavior of droplet burning rate and the transient behavior of flame extinction. There are several suggested mechanisms through which sooting can affect the burning behavior. The formation of soot through pyrolysis reactions (pyrolysis reactions are strongly endothermic) is a heat sink (Choi et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
(1990b);Jackson et al. (1992)). This factor will reduce the effective heat of combustion and therefore the transfer number. Although the transfer number appears in the natural log term of the d2 -law, reduction in the burning rate is expected for conditions of significant soot conversion. Related to this phenomenon is the accumulation of the formed soot in a sootshell. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
This material contains chemical energy not released in the flame and thus causes a reduction in the effective heat of combustion (Choi et al. (1990b);Jackson et al. (1991)). The magnitude of this effect is related to the soot conversion ratio (mass of soot formed divided by mass of fuel consumed). The presence of soot can also modify the thermophysical properties of the soot-laden gas. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg16-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 16 [part 3/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The thermal conductivity is higher for soot compared to the surrounding gas, but the overall conductivity of the mixture is not expected to be much different from the gas since contact among soot aggregates to form a solid structural shell is unlikely. The presence of the soot in the fuel-rich region can also affect the radiative emission and reabsorption, thereby influencing the flame temperature distribution. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
Since soot is a broad band emitter, its presence between the droplet surface and the flame could influence radiative heat loss from the flame zone. The enhanced soot radiation near flame zone could lead to droplet heat up by liquid phase absorption or an overall reduction in heat transferred back to the droplet caused by reduced flame temperature and absorption of the radiation from the flame by the soot-containing region. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
When the loss mechanism dominates the flame temperature will be lowered sufficiently and cause radiative extinction of the flame as observed by Nayagam et al. (1998). A comprehensive theoretical or numerical model, which includes soot formation process (chemical and physical aspects) and radiation, that can predict radiative extinction is still not available. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg16-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 16 [part 4/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Manzello et al. (2000) have included the luminous radiation from soot in their numerical calculations by including the measured soot volume fraction values. Figure 5 displays the measured and computed burning rates for various initial droplet diameters. More recently, Kumar et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
(2002) have presented a soot model that includes soot nucleation, growth, and coagulation and O2 and OH oxidation for transient combustion of spherically symmetric n-heptane droplets. Understanding soot formation is also important in fire safety applications. Fire sensing tech-nology in microgravity environments typically relies on scattering from particles(Urban et al. (1997)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
Characterization of soot morphological properties including the primary particle size, dp , radius of gyration, Rg , and fractal dimension, Df r, and their evolution with residence time under various environmental conditions are important for the analysis of soot processes including primary particle growth, soot agglomeration and oxidation processes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg16-5
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 16 [part 5/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Despite the progress that has been made over the past several decades, systematic detailed measurements of soot properties over a wide range of initial and ambient conditions that could be used to develop and validate theoretical and numerical models are not yet available. The proposed soot measurements in FLEX-2 investigation should provide some of these data for at [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]
10 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 16]

2.5 Slow Convective Effects [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 17]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg17-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 17 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

As described in the previous section droplet combustion involves interaction among different time scales, namely residence time, chemical time, radiation time, etc. Often, in many practical applications there exists a relative motion between the droplet and surrounding gas. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 17]
Forced convective flow imposed on a burning droplet introduces an additional time scale (∼ d/U∞ ) , where U∞ is the relative gas/droplet velocity and d is the droplet diameter. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 17]
This fluid me-chanical time scale interacts with other characteristic time scales (e.g., liquid and gas phase diffusion, chemical kinetic) and modifies the results of quiescent droplet combustion for burning rates, flame dynamics, soot and other pollution formation, as well as ignition and extinction conditions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 17]
The most visible demonstration of this fact is that when a droplet burns in a con-vective environment it can support several different flame configurations, namely, an envelope flame, side-boundary-layer flame, and wake flame, unlike the quiescent burning case where only an envelope flame is possible. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 17]

-----

Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg18-2

defined as Re = dU∞ /ν∞ where ν∞ is the kinematic viscosity of the oxidizer at the ambient temperature is of the order one or less, has received very little attention in the past. Most of the results available thus far rely heavily on semi-empirical approach resulting in correlations of experimental measurements and numerical simulations (Chiu (2000)). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]
The classical works of Frossling (1938), and Ranz and Marshall (1952) were concerned with the evaporation rates of fuel droplets in a flowing stream. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]
The well-known Frossling Ranz-Marshall (FRM) correlation, N u = 2.0K1P rmRen (1) where N u = hd/k is the Nusselt number, P r = α/ν is the Prandtl number, α is the thermal diffusivity, k is the thermal conductivity, h is the heat transfer coefficient, and the exponents m and n are 1/3 and 1/2, respectively, and the constant K1 = 0.6, is originally developed for evaporating droplets. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]
Numerous studies since then have developed several ad-hoc techniques to account for the high gasification rates and the thermophysical property variations during combustion. These semi-empirical correlations include those of Eisenklam et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg18-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 18 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

(1967), Law and Williams (1972) and Sirignano (1999). The major draw-back of these semi-empirical correlations is that they lack universal validity and are limited to certain range of conditions. While these approximate analyses provide a useful way to make complex spray calculations, they do not provide concrete physical predictions to test against experimental measurements. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]
Fairly sophisticated numerical simulations of convective droplet combustion have been developed in the last decade. The key contribution of these numerical models is the inclusion of full Navier-Stokes equations for the gas-phase flow field. However, due to the inherent complexity of the problem, varying degrees of approximations have been made in treating the chemistry, radiation, and the coupling between the gas and liquid phases. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]
Dwyer and Sanders (1986, 1988) have presented unsteady numerical calculations for a single droplet burning in a flowing stream for Reynolds number ranging from 100 initially to small values. Huang and Chen (1998) numerically solved conservation equations for steady burning of a fuel droplet under buoyancy induced flow conditions (normal gravity conditions). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 18]

and Gogos (2005); Raghavan et al. (2006) have developed a comprehensive numerical code capable of simulating both the liquid-phase and solid-phase processes during droplet combustion in slow convective flows. Their numerical code at this time can not simulate combustion of large droplets or sooting droplets where radiation and soot formation become important. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 19]
Theoretical treatments of slow convective droplet combustion stem from the fluid dynamic studies on the motion of a spherical particle in a quiescent environment. In these studies, the convective effect is considered as a perturbation to the spherically symmetric combustion and a suitable small perturbation parameter is identified. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 19]
The gas-phase conservation equations are then expanded in terms of this small parameter to obtain a perturbation problem to be solved using method of matched asymptotic expansions. Fendell and co-workers (Fendell et al. (1966)) were the first to investigate the effects of forced convection on droplet burning. In their analysis the small perturbation parameter is the Peclet number P e(= U∞ d/D << 1). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 19]
Ayyaswamy and co-workers (Sadhal and Ayyaswamy (1983); Gogos et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 19]

(1966) and developed a perturbation solution using Reynolds number as a perturbation parameter. Recently Ackerman and WIlliams (2005) developed an asymptotic model for droplet combustion valid for small Reynolds number regime. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 19]
Okajima and Kumagai (1974b, 1982) were the first to report experimental results for fuel droplets burning in a slow convective flow. Their experiments were carried out in a microgravity envi-ronment (drop-tower) at atmospheric pressures using a wind tunnel. In order to develop a comprehensive theoretical and numerical models for convective droplet combustion detailed measurements over a range of initial conditions are needed. 2.6 Droplet Arrays Combustion [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg20-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 20 [part 4/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Studying small numbers of interacting droplets in a well-controlled geometry represents a logical step in extending single droplet investigations to more practical spray configurations. Studies of droplet interactions date back to Rex et al. (1956) , and were most recently summarized by Annamalai and Ryan (1992) and Annamalai (1995). Most studies determined the change in the burning rate constant, K, as a result of interactions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]
The changes in K occur as a result of two competing mechanisms. The burning droplets compete for the available oxidizer, but there is also a reduction in the heat loss from the flame. Under certain conditions, there exists a separation distance where the droplet lifetime reaches a minimum (Rex et al., 1956; Sangiovanni and Kesten, 1976; Xiong et al., 1984). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]
Additionally, since inter-droplet separation distance, L, increases relative to the droplet size, D, as the burning proceeds, the burning rate is not constant throughout the burn, but changes continuously with time (Miyasaka and Law, 1981; Xiong et al., 1984). These experiments were all in normal gravity, and the strong buoyant flow strongly influenced the observations. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg20-5
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 20 [part 5/5]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The majority of the experimental studies cited were in terrestrial laboratories. Both Law and co-workers (Miyasaka and Law, 1981; Xiong et al., 1984) and Mikami and co-workers (Mikami et al., 1994), however, did perform their experiments in reduced buoyancy environments pro-duced using reduced pressure conditions. Law and co-workers utilized low pressure, high oxygen ambients, and Mikami and co-workers used drop towers. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]
The oxidizer concentration in the am-bient had to be high (> 40%) in Lawś studies, however, to avoid extinction or ignition limits due to the reduced pressure. Both of these studies concluded that interactions retarded droplet heating early in the droplet lifetime. Mikami and co-workers further found that the burn time of an array reached a minimum at a critical inter-droplet spacing. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]
They assert that this minimum results from the opposite effects of radiative enhanced heating of the droplets and competition [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]
14 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 20]

for the available oxygen. While both diffusive and radiative extinction of single droplets in microgravity have been observed before (Dietrich et al. (1996); Nayagam et al. (1998), there exists almost no information on how droplet interactions affect the extinction limits. Figure 8 shows the burning behavior for a single droplet and a binary array (L ≈ 3 mm) in a 0.15 oxygen mole fraction (balance nitrogen), 190 mmHg ambient. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 21]
There are several noteworthy features of the single droplet burning behavior. First, this ambient condition was not flammable in normal gravity. In fact, in this ambient oxygen mole fraction, droplets of this size could not sustain a flame in normal gravity at pressures up to 760 mmHg. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 21]
Figure 8 also shows that while the single droplet burned to completion, the flame surrounding the array extinguished approximately one second after the igniter withdrew. The droplet diameter at flame extinction was approximately 1.1 mm for both droplets of the array. Further, the flame size was nearly 50 percent larger than the flame surrounding the single droplet and the flame was much dimmer (evaluated by the intensity of the video images). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 21]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg21-3

In the binary array test, the flame size increased, reached a maximum, and extinguished. The above trends were consistent over a range of inter-droplet spacings and ambient pressures. In the 0.15 oxygen mole fraction ambient, the flame surrounding the binary array always extinguished at a finite droplet size that was larger than the single droplet extinction diameter. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 21]
Figure 9 shows a similar results for a single and binary droplet array burning in oxygen/helium environment. The burning rate constant K was much higher for the helium diluted experiments than those in the nitrogen diluted experiments (Fig. 8). Also, the flames were much brighter [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 21]

in the He/O2 ambients and the flame sizes and standoff distances in the He/O2 ambients were approximately 50 percent smaller. The flame surrounding the single droplet extinguishes quickly after ignition (Dext ≈ 1.4 mm). The flame surrounding the binary array, however, burns much longer, and the extinction droplet diameter is much smaller (Dext ≈ 1.1 mm). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 22]

Further tests showed that the flames surrounding the binary droplet array at inter-droplet spacings of L ≈ 8 and 12 mm (merged flames existed for both spacings) both extinguished at droplet diameters smaller than the single droplet.
