### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg22-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 22 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The helium and nitrogen-diluted microgravity experiments clearly exhibit different extinction behavior for the binary arrays versus the single droplets. The single droplets and binary arrays in the nitrogen ambient have very large, weak flames and small burning rate constants (relative to the helium-diluted tests). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 22]
Consequently, the ratio of the radiative heat loss from the flame zone to the energy release from combustion is likely much larger for the nitrogen-diluted tests. This ratio increases with droplet size since the radiative loss increases with the cube of the droplet diameter while the heat release increases proportionally to the droplet diameter (assuming K is independent of droplet diameter). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 22]
Therefore one could speculate that the increase in flame [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 22]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg23-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 23 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

size, and decrease in K from the single droplet to the binary droplet array increases the ratio of radiative loss to energy release, potentially enough to cause extinction of the binary array. Alternately, if the inter-droplet spacing were to approach zero for a binary droplet array, this would create a single droplet whose size is 26 percent larger than each individual droplet. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 23]
This increase in droplet size and corresponding increase in radiative loss relative to heat release could be enough to cause extinction of the flame surrounding the binary array. A similar argument can be made to explain the behavior of the helium-diluted tests. The decrease in flame size, increase in K, and the results of previous work Nayagam et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 23]
(1998) lead us to believe that the helium-diluted experiments were less dominated by radiative loss. Since radiative loss is not dominant, the flame temperature is (approximately) independent of the flame size (Williams, 1985). Extinction occurs when the residence time is smaller than the required chemical reaction time, the later of which is independent of droplet diameter in this regime (diffusive extinction). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 23]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg23-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 23 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

For the binary droplet array, if the inter-droplet spacing were zero, this would again create a larger single droplet, with a correspondingly larger flame. This single droplet would have a larger residence time, and the flame would not extinguish until the droplet size was equal to the single droplet extinction size. The data in Fig 9 support this view. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 23]
If the two droplets in the array at flame extinction merged, the resulting single droplet would have a droplet size very close to the extinction droplet size in the single droplet test. Almost no theoretical or numerical studies exist to compare with the present experiments. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 23]

3.0 Research Goals FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 3 Research Goals 3.1 Liquid-Phase Phenomena [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg24-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 24 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

As described in the previous section the overall objective of the bi-component droplet combustion experiments is to examine the role of liquid phase mixing on the burning characteristics of a single droplet. Liquid phase mixing caused either by thermocapillary flows or by solutalcapillary flows will be investigated. The experiments will examine the burning behavior of single droplets over a range of the param-eter ε. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
This is accomplished by examining different fuel mixtures, ambient pressures, oxygen mole fractions, and also altering the time from deployment to ignition (altering the initial liquid motion in the droplet). The flight experiments will involve two different classes of bi-component fuel mixture droplets. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
The first class is expected from theoretical predictions to be hydrody-namically stable in the sense that droplet internal flows will be damped as a result of stabilizing thermocapillary effects. Alkane fuel mixtures, e.g., heptane and hexadecane, are candidate fuels for these experiments. The heptane-hexadecane droplets will display flame contractions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg24-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 24 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

In addition, differences in surface tension between heptane and hexadecane are small enough such that solutalcapillary effects should be subdominant and these droplets should be hydrodynam-ically stable. For these droplets, thermocapillary effects should damp internal droplet flows, as suggested by Aharon and Shaw (1996). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
The second class of fuel mixture droplets is expected to be hydrodynamically unstable in that droplet internal flows will be strongly promoted as a result of destabilizing solutal-capillary effects that are expected to cause rapid motions in the liquid phase, causing significant mix-ing within droplets. Candidate fuels for these experiments are 1-propanol and glycerol, which will not absorb significant amounts of water. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
The propanol-glycerol droplets will display flame contractions as well. However, the surface tension for propanol is much smaller than for glyc-erol, which should induce hydrodynamic instabilities, i.e., capillary flows from solutalcapillary effects, as suggested by the theory of Aharon and Shaw (1996). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg24-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 24 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

In addition, these droplets may absorb small amounts of water from the ambient during combustion - this water will also induce solutalcapillary instabilities. Any absorbed water will be completely miscible with the propanol-glycerol droplet mixture. Complete miscibility is important to allow comparison with present theory. The ambient pressure changes the boiling temperature of the fuel. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
The burning rate constant, however, is only a weak function of the pressure. Therefore, by changing the pressure, one can change the ratio of the liquid phase diffusion coefficient to the burning rate constant (the small parameter, ε, in the analyses of Shaw and Williams (1990) and Aharon and Shaw (1998). Estimates indicate that the change in pressure from 1 to 3 atm will result in about a factor of 5 increase in ε. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
Changes in the ambient composition will cause changes in burning rates by about 50%. In addition, varying the levels of internal circulation within droplets will change effective liquid diffusivities by at least a factor of 2. All of these factors will be allowed to vary by more than an order of magnitude (e.g., from 0.01 to 0.5). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]
18 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 24]

3.2 Surrogate Fuel Droplet Combustion FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 3.2 Surrogate Fuel Droplet Combustion [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg25-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 25 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

This area of study involves application of the one-dimensional droplet flame configuration shown in Fig.4 to evaluate the combustion process of hydrocarbons considered as surrogate components of a real transportation fuel such as JP8. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]
The idea is that the spherical droplet flame configura-tion should be as useful for developing surrogate liquid fuels as counterflow flames, shock tubes, pool fires, stirred reactors, and flow reactors have been to evaluate performance of gaseous fuels. Each of these configurations provides a unique perspective to combustion by targeting specific aspects of the burning process, for example combustion chemistry, ignition and extinction. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]
Fur-thermore, unlike these other combustion configurations, the spherical droplet flame is important because it captures some of the unsteadiness which is typical of combustion of liquid fuels (i.e., sprays) Several considerations guide the selection of the liquids for this study. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]
They concern the chemi-cal and physical characteristics of JP8 which is comprised of the following general hydrocarbon classifications: straight chain alkanes, aromatics, branched-chain alkanes, and single ring aro-matics (Edwards and Maurice (2001). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]

-----

The boiling point ranges of the liquids in Table 1 are close enough that microexplosions should not be expected to occur for blends of the liquids. This is an important consideration because of the droplet deployment design which will be used in the proposed experiments. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]
Three mixtures are also included in the study: heptane/octane mixture (which has long been considered as a surrogate for gasoline since the 1920s); decane/propylbenzne (currently under consideration in Europe as a surrogate (Colket et al. (2007)) and JP8/hexanol. Hexanol is included because of its significantly reduced sooting propensity while also having a boiling point in the range of the other liquids listed in Table 1. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 25]
With the exception of hexanol, all of the hydrocarbons soot extensively. We expect soot shells to form and for soot shell diameter to be included in the database to be developed as part of the proposed research except possibly for JP8. One of the purposes of hexanol will be to reduce soot formation through blending to allow observation of the droplet through a dense soot cloud. The initial droplet diameters will be in the sub-millimeter range for the ground-based investi-gations. The information from droplets of this size will bridge to larger droplet studies pursued in the FLEX-2 flight experiments. There are a number of aspects of the burning which are droplet-size-dependent, most notably the evolution of droplet diameter and the droplet burning rate, and soot formation (Lee et al. (1998); Jackson et al. (1992); [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 26]
Jackson and Avedisian (1994)), which will benefit from comparisons between large and small droplet results. The ambient will include inerts of nitrogen (e.g., air) and helium or CO2. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 26]
Measurements will be made of the following: evolution of droplet diameter (if soot does not obscure the droplet visibility) and flame structure, burning rate (slope of D2 ), burning time, extinction diameter (if extinction occurs), and soot shell diameter. ### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg27-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 27 [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Convective droplet combustion is inherently a complex problem involving nonlinear interactions among disparate time and length scales. Review of the literature clearly reveals that most of the work in this area has been motivated primarily by spray combustion applications and rely heavily on empirical or semi-empirical methods using limited experimental data and numerical simulations. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 27]
Fundamental physical processes, especially those involving transient phenomena are still not well understood. Experiments involving controlled transient effects including droplet acceleration/deceleration and extinction are scarce if not nonexistent. In the low Reynolds number regime, with envelope flames, there are a limited number of microgravity experimental data obtained in air at atmospheric pressures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 27]
In order to develop comprehensive theoretical models and validate them, more systematic experimentation over wider range of parameters is needed (Avedisian (2000)). In the modeling arena both numerical simulations and theoretical models which use asymptotic techniques are available for convective droplet combustion. However, these models include a [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 27]
21 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 27]

3.5 Droplet Arrays Combustion FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]
variety of simplifying assumptions and are in need of more comprehensive comparisons against experiments. Transient phenomena including extinction and acceleration/deceleration effects have scarcely been addressed in the past. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]
The present project is aimed at obtaining systematic data on convective droplet combustion in the small Reynolds number regime over a range of droplet and environmental conditions and should help in the development of comprehensive theoretical models. Specific objectives of the slow convective droplet combustion study are described in the next section. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg28-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 28 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The droplet arrays experiments described in the previous section provided unique insight into the extinction behavior of droplet arrays compared to single droplets. The data, however, were limited by the time available in the microgravity facilities. Ideally, there would be sufficient test time to observe both radiative and diffusive extinction in a single test ambient (nitrogen or helium). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]
Ground-based microgravity facilities do not have sufficient reduced gravity time (drop towers) or quality (aircraft) to get data over a range of droplet sizes and ambient environments. The proposed experiment will examine the burning behavior of binary droplet arrays over a range of droplet sizes, droplet separation distances and ambient environments where it is possible to observe both diffusive and radiative extinction. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]
The goal of the work is to test determine the flammability map of both the single droplet and droplet array and determine how droplet interactions influence the extinction and flammability limits. Figure 12 shows a conceptual flammability diagram of a single droplet and a binary droplet array. The abscissa is the droplet size and the ordinate is the ambient oxygen mole fraction. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg28-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 28 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

For the single droplet the left branch represents the diffusive extinction regime and the right branch represents the radiative extinction regime. Inside this boundary quasi-steady burning is possible, and outside it is not. In a typical droplet combustion experiment ignition occurs at a point inside the boundary, and burning proceeds along a horizontal line up until the diffusive extinction boundary line, where the flame extinguishes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]
The results presented in the previous section suggest that droplet interactions shift this boundary to the left as Fig 12 shows. Details of the experimental procedure and specific experimental objectives are described in the following sections. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 28]

4.0 Flight Experiment Description FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1  Figure 12 – Qualitative representation of the effect of droplet interactions on flammability limits 4 Flight Experiment Description [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 29]
The flight experiment utilizes the Multiple Droplet Combustion Apparatus (MDCA) installed in the Combustion Integrated Rack (CIR) in the International Space Station (ISS) to investigate several fundamental aspects of droplet combustion. Combustion of large droplets with burning times of the order of tens of seconds are the focus of the flight experiments while the ground-based microgravity experiments are focussed on smaller droplets. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 29]
From experimental point of view the overall investigation can be grouped into the following four categories, namely [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 29]
• Pure fuel experiments • Fuel mixture experiments • Convective effects experiments • Droplet arrays experiment [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 29]

This section outlines the specific scientific objectives, the diagnostic requirements, and the science data end products for each of the four categories. 4.1 Experiment Objectives FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 4.1 Experiment Objectives [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
Pure Fuel Experiments: The objectives of the pure fuel studies are to examine the steady and unsteady liquid and gas-phase phenomena, flame extinction, soot formation mechanisms and radiative heat transfer. As explained in the earlier sections, decane (C10 H22 ) and ethanol (C2 H5 OH) fuels are chosen so that we can build on the results of our earlier flight experiments (DCE, FSDC-1 and FSDC-2) with heptane and methanol. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
In these experiments droplet burning rate, flame dimensions, radiative emissions, and soot volume fraction are measured as a function of time and droplet initial size at different ambient conditions. When flame extinction occurs, the diameter of the unburned droplet at extinction is also determined. The specific set of objectives for these experiments are as follows: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg30-2

Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

4.1.1 Determine diffusive and radiative flame extinction boundaries for ethanol and decane droplets in test atmospheres containing N2 or He at pressures ranging from 0.5 atm to 3.0 atm. 4.1.2 Measure droplet extinction diameters for ethanol and decane fuels for validation of detailed and reduced chemical kinetic models. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
4.1.3 Measure soot volume fractions and soot temperatures for a range of initial conditions to determine influence on droplet burning and flame extinction. 4.1.4 Measure radiant emissions from both luminous and non-luminous flames in test atmo-spheres containing N2 or He and at pressures ranging from 0.5 atm to 3.0 atm. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg30-3

Fuel Mixture Experiments: Bi-component fuel mixtures are chosen to examine the role of liq-uid phase transport effects as well as to study the burning characteristics of simulated ‘real’ fuels. Propanol/glycerol and heptane/hexadecane mixtures are used to examine the role of liq-uid phase diffusion, preferential evaporation, and mixing in the burning characteristics of fuel mixtures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
Heptane/octane and decane/propylbenzene experiments are focussed on simulating the characteristics of ‘real’ fuels, like gasoline and jet fuels, which contain a variety of hydrocar-bon with differing volatilities. The experimental measurements made during these experiments are similar to the pure fuel studies, however, the initial fuel composition and the interpretation of the results are different. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
The primary objectives of these experiments are as follows: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]
4.1.5 Measure diameter dc at the onset of flame contraction relative to the initial droplet diam-eter d0 . Correlate with theoretical predictions and provide estimates for effective liquid diffusivities. 4.1.6 Measure burning rate, burning time, extinction diameter, and flame luminosity for the JP8 surrogate fuel mixtures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 30]

4.2 Test Matrix Envelope FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
The imposed flow velocities are such that the Reynolds number based on initial droplet diameter is of the order unity. The specific objectives of the present flight experiments are as follows: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
4.1.7 Obtain radiative and diffusive flame extinction limits in the low Reynolds number flow regime over the range of ambient test pressures and oxygen concentrations. 4.1.8 Measure droplet regression rates for sooting (decane) and non sooting (ethanol) fuels as a function of imposed flow at low velocities over a range of ambient oxygen concentrations and pressures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
4.1.9 Measure flame stand-off distance, flame shape, and temperature field measurements in low flow velocities under a range of test atmospheres for use in validation of detailed theoretical models (i.e., radiative and finite-rate chemical kinetic effects). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg31-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 31 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Droplet Arrays Experiments: The objectives of the droplet arrays experiments are to investigate the droplet-droplet interactions on the diffusive and radiative extinction of the flame surrounding the droplets for various L/D0 ratios using pure fuels. The specific microgravity experimental objectives are as follows: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
4.1.10 Determine the flammability map (diffusive and radiative extinction limits) for a binary droplet array as function of the L/D0 ratio where the droplet spacing will be determined by the bead spacing. 4.1.11 Measure variations in burning rate constant k as a function of L/D0 for a range of ambient conditions to verify theoretical predictions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg31-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 31 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The overall FLEX-2 experiments consist of a series of droplet combustion experiments to ex-amine the fundamental aspects of pure and fuel-mixture droplets. The experiments involve dispensing, deploying and igniting a single droplet in a known and controlled gaseous environ-ment. The ignited droplets burns to either completion or the flame extinguishes at a finite droplet radius in a quiescent ambient. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
During the droplet arrays experiments, two droplets are placed along a support fiber at known locations and the droplets are ignited simultaneously and the subsequent burning is recorded. In the convective flow tests the droplet is subjected to a known and controlled small, sub-buoyant flow. The primary independent experiment variables are described in the next section followed by a discussion of the diagnostics. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
The exact require-ments in tabular form for the hardware and diagnostics are discussed in subsequent sections. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]
4.2 Test Matrix Envelope [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 31]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg39-1

5.1 Experiment Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 Hardware Requirements (cont.) Section Description Requirements - acceleration levels less than 10−4 m/s2 on three axes [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
Microgravity - measurement accuracy shall be 10−6 m/s2 5.1.7 Requirements - frequency range shall be 0.01 − 125 Hz - sampling rate shall be 1 − 5 times the frequency 5.1.1 Test Fuels [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg39-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 39 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

FLEX-2 experiments use two different pure fuels and four different fuel mixtures. The pure fuels are a sooting alkane fuel (decane) and a nominally non-sooting alcohol fuel (ethanol). Investigations of sooting and non-sooting fuels allow us to quantify the influences of sooting on extinction and burning dynamics, such as radiative output. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
Also, the chemical kinetics of alkanes and alcohols are fundamentally different due to the presence of oxygen molecules in the alcohol which will help explain effects of fuels on the burning characteristics. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
In addition, since the Droplet Combustion Experiment 1 (DCE-1) and FLEX-1 experiments used a lower alkane (heptane) and a lower alcohol (methanol) these higher order alkane and alcohol test will help develop comprehensive chemical kinetic models for realistic fuels. These fuels have also been used in the FSDC and drop-tower experiments and extensive experimental data exist for smaller droplets. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
The fuel mixtures used in the experiments are heptane/hexadecane, propanol/glycerol, heptane/octane, and decane/propylbenzene. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg39-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 39 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The frist two binary fuel mixtures are chosen to examine the liquid phase diffusion effects and the last two binary fuel mixtures are used to develop surrogate models for the aviation fuel JP8. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
Fuel type: Pure fuel droplets shall be n-decane (C10 H22 ), an alkane fuel with a wide range of sooting behavior that varies depending on environmental conditions or ethanol (C2 H5 OH), a non-sooting alcohol fuel. Mixed fuel droplet tests shall be selected from any of the following binary fuel mixtures; n-heptane/n-hexadecane, 1-propanol/glycerol, n-heptane/iso-octane, and n-decane/n-propylbenzene. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
Fuel purity: Purity levels shall be the highest purity level that is commercially available (research grade at 99.5% by volume at present) and all liquids shall be degassed and contain no H2 O. Quality assurance: Certification of test samples shall be provided prior to flight. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]
Fuel temperature: Fuels shall be deployed at temperatures between 18 o C − 27 o C. 33 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 39]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg40-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 40 [part 1/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

5.1 Experiment Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 5.1.2 Droplet Deployment and Ignition [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
Droplet sizes, during FLEX-2 experiments, will vary between 1.5 mm and 5.0 mm. A measured quantity of fuel is dispensed from a fuel reservoir and deposited onto the support fiber using a suitable deployment technique (e.g., opposed-needle technique used in DCE or single-needle technique implemented in the ground-based rigs). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
A subset of tests will require that the droplets are deployed on support fibers equipped with either a single pinning bead (i.e., for the slow convective flow tests, as discussed in Section 2.5) or a set of pinning beads spaced a distance, L , apart (i.e., droplet array tests, as discussed in Section 2.6). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
In addition to the subset of tests requiring a beaded fiber it will also be required to provide the option for all tests, at the discretion of the researcher, of deploying droplets onto a beaded or an un-beaded fiber. This option would be exercised when the the researcher feels it is warranted either to ensure a successful droplet deployment or to take advantage of fiber pyrometry. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg40-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 40 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Following droplet deployment, after a preset dwell time designed to allow droplet surface oscil-lations to die out, the fuel vapor/oxidizer mixture is ignited using a minimum ignition energy. Previous experimental results show that deployment-induced droplet surface oscillations die out within a second. The ignition system should be capable of waiting for a predetermined dura-tion for these oscillations to die out. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
Immediately following ignition, the ignitor, as well as all other hardware should be removed from the test region. This zone-of-exclusion is defined as a spherical region extending at least 10 initial droplet diameters in the radial direction from the droplet center. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
Droplet size: Droplet diameters shall vary from 1.5 mm to 5 mm ± 0.25 mm. Droplet reproducibility: Droplet size shall be reproducible to within ± 5%. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]

The material used for the fiber shall have incandescent properties comparable to that possessed by an 80 μm SiC fiber, such that the emission intensity can be calibrated against a blackbody source to allow for indirect temperature measurement using thin filament pyrometry techniques with the existing HiBM’s camera and LCTF configuration. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]
It is requested that the thermal diffusivity of the support fiber be kept as low as possible and, at its worst, no greater than what is curently being used for FLEX (i.e., α = 2.3cm2/s for a SiC fiber). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 40]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg42-1

5.1 Experiment Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 5.1.4 Initial Ambient [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
The ambient oxygen mole fraction influences the burning rate, extinction diameter, flame chem-istry and flame characteristics (size, temperature). This parameter will vary from 0.4 down to the limit where no flame can be sustained at any droplet diameter. The diluents used in the present study will be nitrogen and helium. Note that both nitrogen and helium are bio-compatible and helium help control the flame temperature. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
Oxygen mole fraction: The oxygen mole fraction shall range from 0.1 − 0.4 ± 0.005. Diluent type: Diluents shall consist of either N2 or He. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
Diluent mole fraction: The diluent mole fractions shall vary depending upon the oxygen concentration specified for each test. Ambient quiescence: Gases shall be well-mixed and quiescent prior to ignition. Ambient temperature: The initial chamber gas temperature shall be within 18 o C −27 o C. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
Relative humidity: The chamber relative humidity shall be less than 10% for all the test points. 5.1.5 Droplet Translation [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg42-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 42 [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

One objective of FLEX-2 is to study slow convective flow effects on burning characteristics. The “flow field” may be established around the droplet by translating a tethered droplet to induce an effective flow field. This method would provide precise control over the imposed flow and would allow accurate control of the flow acceleration/deceleration, particularly at low speeds. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
During combustion the droplet continually shrinks in size and the Reynolds number, based on instantaneous droplet diameter, continually decreases if the flow velocity is not increased with time. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
In order to maintain a desired Reynolds number during combustion and to study flow induced transient effects it is requested that the translation velocity is controllable with prescribed acceleration/deceleration values that are selectable for specific test runs. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
Droplet translation velocity: The tethered droplet translation velocity shall be selectable for a given test between 0.1 cm/sec to 5 cm/sec ± 1%. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]
Droplet acceleration/deceleration: The tethered droplet translation acceleration/deceleration shall be selectable for a given test. 36 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 42]

5.1 Experiment Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 5.1.6 Operational Requirements [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg43-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 43 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The chamber shall be filled with the appropriate atmosphere, which will vary in pressure between 0.5 atm to 3.0 atm and will vary in O2 concentration between 0.1 to 0.4 mole fraction with the diluent gas making up the balance. A settling time of approximately 2 minutes will elapse prior to initiating the test in order to ensure that the temperature and pressure of the chamber gases have stabilized. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]
This settling time will be followed by the dispensing of a predetermined amount of fuel (based on the target droplet size) onto the support fiber for those tests in which the droplet is not freely deployed. When sufficient fuel has been dispensed the dispensing needles will be retracted and a dwell period of at least 10 seconds will be allowed for the droplet internal fluid motion induced by deployment to subside. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]
This will then be followed by initiating power to the ignitor for a selectable amount of time ranging from 1 second to 5 seconds after which the ignitor will be retracted from the field of view. If the flow field is to be generated by translating the droplet then droplet motion would commence at the same time that the ignitor is retracted. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg43-4

In order to compare the experimental data with theoretical results obtained under the condi-tions when Re ∼ O(1) we need to minimize the effects of buoyancy on the flow field. The dimensionless parameter that compares the buoyancy effects to forced flow effects is the ratio of two dimensionless groups, the Gr/Re2 , where Gr is the Grashof number, and Re is the Reynolds number. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]
The magnitude of this dimensionless group indicates the relative effect of 1 This time may be insufficient for large droplets, at least for damping of internal flows. Also, internal flows within methanol droplets may never decay to low levels because of Marangoni effects (i.e., from water absorption) [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]
37 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 43]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg44

5.1 Experiment Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 44]
buoyancy compared to forced convection. These experiments will require that Gr/Re2 << 1 which translates into the following requirement for the g-level: Gr g β ΔT D 2 = 2 << 1 (2) Re U∞ [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 44]
where g is the gravitational acceleration, β is the coefficient of thermal expansion, ΔT is the characteristic temperature difference, D is the characteristic length scale and U∞ is the free-stream velocity. For the worst case scenario where β · ΔT ∼ 5, D ∼ 1 cm and U∞ ∼ 1 cm/s which suggests a g/g0 value less than 1x10−5 as the required g-level (where g0 is earth’s normal gravity). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 44]
Micro-gravity levels: Accelerations are required to be less than 10−4 m/s2 within a fre-quency range of 0.01 Hz − 125 Hz in order to ensure buoyant forces are negligible. Mea-surements shall be made within an accuracy of 10−6 m/s2 at a sampling rate of 1 − 5 times the frequency. 38 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 44]

5.2.3 Secondary Color Flame Imaging [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 48]
A color CCD camera viewing the droplet at a specified angle will provide flame color information during combustion. Based on earlier flight experiments, it is also desired that this camera serve as the experiment’s monitor camera to facilitate experiment operation/monitoring from the ground. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 48]
Orientation: The color camera shall provide full view of the droplet and flame with the focal plane parallel to the flow direction preferred; this camera shall provide viewing of the droplet dispensing and deployment sequence. Field of view: The FOV shall be at least 5.0 cm. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 48]
Minimum frame rate: The frame rate shall be 30 f ps over the duration of the test. Downlink: The images shall be down-linked to the PI in near real-time during each test. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 48]
Zoom: A ‘zoom’ capability shall be provided, upon request by the PI (allowing for a commensurate decrease in FOV) in the event that a more detailed view of the deployment, ignition and burning process is required. 42 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 48]

5.2 Diagnostic Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 5.2.4 Flame Radiation [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Prior experimental results from DCE and FSDC have established that both luminous and non-luminous radiation play a significant role in flame extinction. These measurements will be used to obtain the total radiant energy loss as well as to establish the exact moment of flame extinction and to supplement other imaging data in determining transient flame behavior. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Thermopile radiometers, positioned at distances far enough from the droplet to allow full view of the flame, will be used to measure broad-band radiation and water-vapor radiation from the flame zone. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Water vapor: The radiometer used to detect water vapor radiation shall be filtered to detect wavelengths in a band centered at 1.87 μm [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Broad-band spectrum: The radiometer used for broad-band radiation shall detect wave-lengths within a band from 0.6 μm − 5.0 μm. Sample rate: Data sampled from the radiometer shall be at a frequency of 30 Hz. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Positioning: The radiometers shall be positioned at a distance sufficient for detection of all incident radiation from the flame. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg49-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 49 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Field of view: The FOV of at least 65 mm shall be centered on the droplet deployment site. Response time: The radiometers shall have a time constant of less than 40 ms. Accuracy: The radiometers shall be accurate to within 5% of the estimated full scale incident radiation. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
5.2.5 Soot Volume Fraction Measurement [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg49-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 49 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

It has long been established that the presence of soot will influence the droplet burning rate, the flame dynamics, and the flame extinction limits. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
Increased thermal radiation from the soot forming region, impacts on mass and thermal diffusion due to the physical presence of soot acting as a barrier to the diffusive processes, and the changes in thermophysical properties in the fuel-rich region of the diffusion flame due to varying levels of soot concentration all contribute to changes in the dynamics of the droplet burning and extinction behavior. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
In order to assess these influences with the n-heptane droplet tests it is desired to quantify soot concentration levels by obtaining soot volume fraction measurements within the diffusion flame. An established technique for making this measurement has been to use a multi-chord, line-of-sight light extinction measurement coupled with an inversion technique to obtain soot volume fraction distributions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]
This requires a backlight using a collimated laser light source of known intensity and by discriminating that intensity from the flame emission and stray light by using combinations of interference and neutral density filters. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg49-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 49 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

It is anticipated that this measurement, if available, will use the HiBMS camera with the appropriate spectral filtering, typically 660 nm 43 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 49]

5.2 Diagnostic Requirements FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]
with a FWHM (full width half maximum) of 5 nm, and with an appropriate intensity resolution, which typically requires a minimum of 8 bits of dynamic range. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]
Orientation: The camera used for SVF measurements shall provide a full view of the droplet and flame with a preferred orientation such that the focal plane is parallel to the flow direction and coplanar with the droplet center. Back-lit illumination: A collimated light source with a wavelength of 660 nm and a FWHM of 5 nm shall be provided. Frame rate: The frame rate shall be at least 15 f ps. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]

5.2.6 Soot/Fiber Temperature [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg50-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 50 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

In addition to knowing the soot volume fraction it is equally important to know the soot tem-perature. Therefore, it is desired to quantify the soot temperature (and fiber temperature) to obtain quantitative information regarding the broadband radiation due to soot. An established technique for making soot temperature measurements, which has been success-fully implemented in ground-based facilities, is by use of multi-wavelength pyrometry. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]
This measurement will use the CIR HiBMS camera with the Liquid Crystal Tunable Filter (LCTF) since limitations of the CIR/MDCA hardware will preclude two cameras simultaneously imaging different wavelengths. The framing rate of the single HiBMS camera, combined with a rapid temporal response of the LCTF, will make it feasible to make measurements on successive frames at two different wavelengths. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]
Assuming minimal changes in soot volume fraction and temperature during the inter-frame time, it will be possible to measure soot temperature. Additionally, this system will be used to measure the temperature of the support fiber (during non-sooting tests) as a proxy for the gas-phase flame temperature. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]

[Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 50]

5.3 Flight Experiment Test Procedures FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 5.2.7 Tethered Droplet Motion [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
During the low convectv flow tests it is important that the instantaneous relative free-stream velocity is known accurately during the entire combustion process. This relative flow velocity may be established by translating the tethered droplet. Information providing droplet position with time shall be required with a sampling frequency of at least 30 Hz. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
Sample rate: Droplet position data synchronized with time shall be provided at a fre-quency of at least 30 Hz. 5.2.8 Ambient Temperature and Pressure Measurement [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
During each test the chamber gas temperature and pressure measurements shall be required. The following are the specifications for this data. Sample rate: The frequency of chamber temperature and pressure data shall be 10 Hz. Temperature: Temperatures shall be accurate to within ± 0.5o C. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
Pressure: Pressures shall be accurate to within ± 0.01 atm in the range of 0.5 − 3.0 atm. 5.2.9 Synchronization [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg51-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 51 [part 2/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Experiment objectives require all data to be time-synchronized to a reference time. This will allow accurate interpretation of the data and will allow evaluation of droplet regression rate, flame structure, and flame extinction as a function of time. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
Time synchronization: All measurements shall be referenced to GMT and shall be accu-rate to within ± 0.03 sec. 5.3 Flight Experiment Test Procedures [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
The FLEX-2 experiments involve igniting and observing the burning behavior of relatively large droplets in a preselected ambient condition. The primary objectives of the experiments are to study the fundamental burning characteristics of sooting and non-sooting pure fuels and fuel mixtures. The effects of slow convection and the droplet-droplet interactions associated with droplet pairs are also examined. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
The basic procedures of a typical experimental run will be as follows: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]
1. Select the test pressure, oxygen/diluent mixture concentration and fill the combustion chamber. 45 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 51]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg52-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 52 [part 1/2]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

5.4 FLEX-2 Test Matrix FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 2. Allow, at a minimum, approximately two minutes for the ambient gas to reach quiescence. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]
3. Energize the diagnostic devices and start the recording process and ensure that there is a live down-link of the color camera 4. Select the droplet’s initial size and deploy the droplet (or droplets) in either a free-floated or tethered mode. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]
5. Wait few seconds for droplet deployment induced velocities to die down. 6. Ignite and observe the combustion process. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]
7. If the chamber environment, following a combustion event, permits a subsequent run then repeat steps 3 through 6; if not, then start from step 1. 5.4 FLEX-2 Test Matrix [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]

A detailed test matrix is currently under development and will need to strike a balance between meeting all the stated science objectives and the resource and operational constraints imposed by the flight hardware and space station operations. As is customary, the exact test matrix and/or the sequence of test points will potentially change during the conduct of the FLEX-2 experiment. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]
The extensive ground testing in the drop towers at the NASA Glenn Research Center and the results from the earlier FLEX experiments will provide information which will be used in finalizing the final test matrix and test point sequencing. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]
Nonetheless, since it is necessary for the engineering team to have a preliminary test matrix envelope, in order to estimate consumables and prepare for the necessary safety reviews, a test matrix envelop has been presented in Section 4.3. A final test matrix, with individual test point specificity, will be incorporated by addendum to this document in the the Appendix. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 52]

6.0 Post-Flight Data Analysis FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 6 Post-Flight Data Analysis [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 53]
The data obtained from the flight experiments are in the form of video images and text data sampled and stored at a specific time intervals. The video images consist of back-lit droplet view, OH* emission views, and color camera views, all obtained at 30 fps. However, when needed the droplet view camera will be operated up to 100 fps. The radiometer data is stored in text form and sampled at 100 Hz. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 53]
The combustion chamber temperature and pressure are also measured and stored during each experimental run and they are available for data interpretation if needed. Form these raw data a number of quantitative flame characteristics are extracted using image analysis and other mathematical analysis tools. Specific analyses and model(s)-to-experiment comparisons to be performed include, but are not limited to the following list: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 53]
• Droplet diameter as a function time and burning rate constant • Flame diameter as a function of time • Droplet diameter at flame extinction • Flame diameter at extinction • Soot volume fraction as a function of time [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 53]

[Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 53]

6.2 Hardware Success Criteria FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 6.1.1 Minimal Success [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 54]
In order for the flight experiment to be considered minimally successful, the FLEX-2 team must acquire a minimal set of data to allow validation of the theoretical/numerical models over a minimal range of ambient conditions. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 54]
There must be sufficient data to allow for at least two of the four categories of experiments, namely, pure fuel experiments, fuel mixture experiments, convective effects experiments, and droplet arrays experiments, have the following measurements: [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 54]
• Obtain droplet diameter and either OH∗ or color video flame measurements as function of time at two pressures over a range of droplet sizes and oxygen concentrations. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 54]
This criteria essentially states that sufficient data is collected for two of the four categories of experiments to make meaningful scientific conclusions in the form of a technical publication. 6.1.2 Complete Success [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 54]

7.0 Ground-Based Research Program FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 7 Ground-Based Research Program [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
The ground based experimental and theoretical studies form a subset of the overall FLEX-2 flight experimental program. The ground-based studies help in interpreting the flight results over an wider range of time and length scales pertinent to droplet combustion. The following subsections outline the planned experimental and analytical research in support of FLEX-2. There are four primary objectives to the ground-based FLEX research program. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
The first goal is to use the drop towers and reduced gravity aircraft (when appropriate) to develop the procedures and hardware settings necessary to ensure satisfactory operation on orbit. This portion of the ground-based program is typically handled by the engineering team and is not covered by this document. The second component is to take data that will be used to determine the final FLEX-2 test matrix. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
The third component of the ground-based research program is to perform experiments to compliment the flight-based data (typically with smaller initial droplet sizes). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg56-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 56 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The final component of the ground-based research program is the development of the theoretical and numerical tools necessary to extend and generalize the results of droplet combustion experiments to a wider range of fuels and environments. 7.1 Experiments [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
Currently three drop-tower capable (2.2 second tower and the 5 second zero gravity facility) microgravity droplet combustion experimental rigs are available to support the FLEX-2 ex-perimental program, namely, Bi-component rig (UC, Davis), Sooting droplet rig (Drexel), and Convective droplet rig (NASA/NCSER). These rigs are capable of freely deploying the droplet and igniting them at various pressures and ambient environments. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
Tethered droplets with an imposed slow convective velocities can also be studied using one of the rigs. Modification will be made to the droplet translating, fiber-supported droplet combustion rig to study droplet array combustion. An additional experimental rig suitable for the one second drop facility at Cornell will be modified and fitted with digital imaging systems for experiments at Cornell focussed on surrogate fuel studies. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
7.2 Theoretical and Numerical Studies [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg56-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 56 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Theoretical and numerical models help to generalize the experimental observation to a wider range of conditions encountered in practical applications. As described in Section 3, each team member has specific scientific objectives and is responsible for the theoretical and/or numerical model development. Some of the planned activity in this area are described below. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
FLEX-2 research program will examine diffusive transport in detail with the goal of validating and improving diffusive transport models for use in theoretical and numerical simulations of droplet combustion with multicomponent liquid fuels. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
Particular emphases will be to examine the influences of Soret transport and multicomponent diffusion effects on transport of species to and from flame zones, e.g., through appropriate modeling of Lewis numbers, with the resulting [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]
50 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 56]

influences on transient combustion behaviors (e.g., burning rates and flame standoff ratios), radiant heat transfer, extinction, LOI conditions and sooting. The modeling approaches will be analytical, using asymptotic methods, as well as computational, where detailed models for transport will be included in existing codes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 57]
Where appropriate, reduced chemical kinetic models for the various fuels will be developed and expanded as done in the previous flight experiment programs (DCE and FLEX). Numerical models with detailed chemistry along with experimental data will be used to validate the reduced chemical kinetic schemes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 57]
Theoretical models for other aspects of droplet combustion such as the effects of slow convection and droplet-droplet interactions will also be developed during this study. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 57]

8.0 Science Management Plan FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 8 Science Management Plan [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 58]
The FLEX-2 Research Team Lead, F.A. Williams, maintains overall responsibility for the FLEX-2 research program. All major decisions regarding the FLEX-2 flight experiment and ground-based research program rest with the Research Team Lead. It is fully expected, however, that all of the external and internal GRC investigators will have a great deal of autonomy in the day to day operations of their own research. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 58]
This includes the preparation of test plans and conducting experiments and analyzing and reporting the resulting data. All of the participating will meet and talk periodically to discuss flight hardware developments, research results and discuss relevant issues. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 58]
All team members will report the results of their results to NASA in the form of periodic progress reports and more importantly to the peer community in technical meetings and journal publications. The Research Team Lead is responsible for the reporting the results of the overall research at the conclusion of the FLEX-2 project. Table shows the primary responsibility of each of the team members. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 58]

The primary day to day responsibility for the flight hardware rests with the GRC investigators, V. Nayagam and M. Hicks. ### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg60-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 60 [part 1/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

9.0 Justification for Reduced Gravity FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 9 Justification for Reduced Gravity [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg60-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 60 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

The objective of this experiment is to use the droplet as a model geometry for investigating suppressant agent efficacy. In order to completely leverage the droplet geometry, it is critically important to maintain spherical symmetry throughout the test. This can only occur if the diffusive residence times (i.e., mass (τm ) and thermal (τk ) are much lower than the buoyancy-controlled residence time (τb ). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
Using the droplet diameter (D) as the characteristic length the expressions for the ratio of these two characteristic times to the buoyancy controlled residence time are  τk g D3 = (3) τb αg and  τm g D3 = (4) τb Dg where αg and Dg are the thermal and mass diffusivities of the gas phase. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg60-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 60 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

In order to justify neglecting buoyant effects the above ratios must be much lower than unity. This is only accom-plished by changing at least one of the three independent variables, droplet diameter, pressure or gravity level (or some combination of the three). Experiments in 1-g environments, performed in pressures of 1 atmosphere, require droplet diam-eters substantially smaller than 1 mm. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
This makes key diagnostics impractical since the image resolution begins to approach the dimensions of the distances being measured (e.g., flame stand-offs, droplet regression, thin filament pyrometry). Also, the present experiment requires finite and measurable extinction droplet diameters (and a period of near quasi-steady burning prior to extinction) in order to quantify the influence of finite rate chemistry. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
The droplet diameters required to minimize buoyancy effects are small enough that extinction droplet diameters would either not be practically measurable or, more likely, not even exist. Since diffusivities are inversely proportional to pressure the residence time ratios could alter-natively be reduced by reducing test pressures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg60-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 60 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Chung and Law (1986) used this approach to measure extinction droplet diameters and from that determine single step chemical kinetic constants for decane in normal gravity. The range of oxygen concentrations and pressures was, however, very limited. Studying such a small range of droplet diameters, pressures and oxygen concentrations would severely limit the ability to achieve the stated objectives of the present study. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
Furthermore, Easton (1998) showed that the conditions that yielded finite extinction diameters in the work of Chung and Law (1986) burned to completion (no flame extinction) in microgravity. The author attributed this to a small residual buoyant-flow that is large enough in the vicinity of the flame to influence the extinction process (Struk et al., 1997). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
The need for extended duration microgravity facilities is predicated on the fact that the burn-to-completion time for large droplets is longer than the time available in the ground-based [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]
54 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 60]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg61-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 61 [part 1/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

9.0 Justification for Reduced Gravity FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 61]

### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg61-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 61 [part 2/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

facilities. Typically the droplet life-time (τl ) can be estimated from the initial droplet size D2 (D0 ) and the average burning rate constant (k) as τl = k0 . The values of τl for n-heptane, and methanol droplets burning in air at one atmospheric pressure range from 10 to 30 s for initial droplet diameters in the range 3 to 5 mm assuming an average burning rate constant of 0.8 mm2 /s. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 61]
Moreover, high fidelity experiments demand additional microgravity time for droplet deployment, droplet quiescence, and ignition. Experiments involving radiative extinction require that the droplets be ignited in microgravity. Such experimental requirements also lead us to employ larger droplets, which in turn necessitates long-duration microgravity facilities. Both Dietrich et al. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 61]
(2005) and Easton (1998) studied extinction of single droplets in drop towers. Dietrich et al. (2005) used the Japan Microgravity Center 10 s drop tower, a facility no longer available, and even then could only study a limited parameter space because of the limited microgravity time. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 61]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-nohypertext-pg61-3

Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf — page 61 [part 3/3]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf

Therefore, the only facility which will enable data of sufficient quality over a wide parameter space is the microgravity environment available in extended-duration microgravity facilities (i.e. Space Shuttle or ISS). 55 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final _ No Hypertext .pdf, page 61]

Data are from Aharon and Shaw (1998). . . . . . . . . . . 6 2 The ratio Vc /V0 vs. the initial hexadecane mass fraction Y, where Vc is the droplet volume at the onset of flame contraction, and V0 the initial droplet vol-ume. ### flex2-doc-srd-pg7-1
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf — page 7 [part 1/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf

1.0 Introduction FLEX-2 SRD Rev. 1 1 Introduction [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Combustion, which has been both friend and foe to mankind since antiquity, still defies compre-hension in many important respects. Needs therefore exist to advance fundamental knowledge in combustion science. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Practical consequences of such advancement of knowledge are improve-ments in strategies for energy production, since most of the energy that is used by society will continue to be provided by combustion for many years, and mitigation of fire losses, fire safety being a continuing concern in all sectors. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
The NASA Microgravity Combustion Science Program, which was established through recognition of these needs, did much to increase our fundamental understanding of combustion processes Ronney (1988),Williams (2007). Droplet combustion experiments had a great deal to do with the advancements in knowledge through this program. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Such experiments, in effect, focus on a microcosm of the field of combustion that can be used to study most aspects of the topic. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-pg7-2
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf — page 7 [part 2/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf

In addition, droplet combustion is of practical interest in itself, since liquid fuels possess high energy content per unit mass and are relatively safe to store and transport, making them valuable to the transportation industry for the foreseeable future. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Phenomena of finite-rate gas-phase chemistry, multicomponent-species gas-phase transport processes, production of soot and other pollutants, phase-change processes, liquid-phase species separation and fluid motion, natural and force convection, radiation and conductive energy transfer and interactions of different burning fuel elements, for example, all are involved in droplet combustion (and, in fact, all are aspects of the subject of the [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
present SRD). By offering a well-controlled arena in which to investigate these combustion phenomena, microgravity droplet combustion research provides valuable data that help develop detailed chemical and physical models of combustion phenomena. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-pg7-3
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf — page 7 [part 3/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf

The NASA program in the area of microgravity droplet combustion has employed laboratory experiments, low-gravity facilities, aircraft flying parabolic trajectories and space-based research to reduce or eliminate buoyancy, thereby increasing the fidelity of the descriptions of the com-bustion processes. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Following glovebox space-shuttle experiments on a fiber-supported droplet combustion, FSDC, the freely deployed droplet-combustion experiment DCE was flown on the MSL-1 missions, providing a number of new insights into the fundamentals of droplet com-bustion. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
Among the DCE results was the demonstration of boundaries between radiative and diffusive extinction of combustion in droplet burning and the establishment of atmospheres in which the dilution was great enough that the oxygen concentration was too low for combustion to occur Nayagam et al. (1998), Ackerman et al. (2003). [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]

-----
### flex2-doc-srd-pg7-4
Investigation: FLEX-2, Flame Extinguishment Experiment-2 (PSI-68)
Topic: FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf — page 7 [part 4/4]
Sources: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf

This last observation demonstrated how droplet-combustion studies could be used to investigate the limiting oxygen index, LOI, needed to prevent combustion, and so the Flammability-limit experiment FLEX, was designed for the International Space Station, ISS, to be run using the multiuser droplet combustion ap-paratus, MDCA, in the combustion integrated rack, CIR. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
While FLEX is focussed on spacecraft fire safety and fire suppression, FLEX-2 returns to the broader objective of improving the fun-damental understanding of liquid-fuel combustion. The FLEX-2 experiments thus fall in the category of the broad NASA mission to improve basic science through microgravity research. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
They focus on ethanol and decane as fuels, as well as various fuel mixtures, in oxygen-nitrogen and oxygen-helium atmospheres at various pressures. [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]
1 [Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 7]

[Source: Flex2/FLEX-2 SRD Final.pdf, page 8]
